Title: Subsampled Pseudo-posteriors for Scalable Bayesian Moment-condition Inference

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.05308v1

Published Time: Mon, 07 Sep 2026 01:19:33 GMT

Number of Pages: 146

Markdown Content:
# Subsampled Pseudo-posteriors for Scalable Bayesian Moment-condition Inference 

Wenshuo Zhao ∗ and Rong Tang †

Department of Mathematics, Hong Kong University of Science and Technology, Hong Kong. 

Abstract 

Bayesian pseudo-posteriors based on moment conditions, such as Bayesian empir-ical likelihood (EL) and Bayesian exponentially tilted empirical likelihood (ETEL), provide a robust route to Bayesian inference when the model is specified only through moment restrictions, but their computation is often prohibitive. In this work, we address two barriers to Bayesian pseudo-inference under moment restrictions. First, scalable sampling is challenging for tall data because the pseudo-likelihoods are not log-additive across observations, so standard likelihood-subsampling methods do not apply. Second, in simulation-based and latent-variable problems, the moment function itself may be defined through an intractable expectation, making full-data pseudo-posterior inference infeasible. We develop a new family of subsampled pseudo-posteriors that replaces the full-data pseudo-posterior with an aggregate of mini-batch pseudo-posteriors. We show that naive mini-batching induces a shifted-mixture distortion, yielding aggregates that are correctly centered but overly diffuse. To remove this distortion, we introduce moment-function-level control variates that align mini-batch moment equations around a reference estimator and recover the full-data posterior shape. For Bayesian ETEL, we establish finite-sample total varia-tion bounds to the full-data pseudo-posterior, allowing discontinuous and intractable moment functions. Numerical experiments show that the method applies broadly across moment-condition-based Bayesian pseudo-inference, closely approximates full-data pseudo-posteriors, and substantially reduces computational cost. 

Keywords— Bayesian pseudo-inference; Bayesian exponentially tilted empirical likelihood; Intractable moment functions; Discontinuous estimating equations; Subsampled inference. 1arXiv:2609.05308v1 [math.ST] 4 Sep 2026 1 Introduction 

Many statistical models are naturally specified through moment conditions rather than through a fully specified likelihood. In such settings, the parameter of interest is identified by conditions of the form E[g(X, θ )] = 0 , where g(X, θ ) is a vector-valued moment function. Bayesian pseudo-posterior methods [Bissiri et al., 2016, Chernozhukov and Hong, 2003] based on moment conditions provide a way to incorporate prior information while retaining the partial-specification advantages of moment-condition-based inference. They produce natural uncertainty quantification through a posterior distribution and remain useful even in settings with discontinuous moments, where direct frequentist covariance estimation can be delicate. These methods construct pseudo-likelihood surrogates from empirical analogues of the moment restrictions, for example through empirical likelihood (EL) and exponentially tilted empirical likelihood (ETEL) criteria [Owen, 2001, Liu and Zhao, 2023, Qin and Lawless, 1994, Chen et al., 2008, Schennach, 2007]. They have been applied in a wide range of settings, including quantile regression [Yang and He, 2012], survey sampling [Yiu et al., 2020], ridge and lasso regression [Bedoui and Lazar, 2020], generalized-method-of-moments (GMM) inference [Kim, 2002, Yin, 2009], and risk-minimization problems [Tang and Yang, 2022]. Despite their broad applicability, moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors are often computationally challenging. We focus on two barriers in this study. The first is the 

data-size barrier . For tall data, evaluating a moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihood at each value of θ typically requires a full pass through all n observations and, for EL-type methods, the solution of a global constrained optimization problem. In some settings the cost is even superlinear. For example, in Wilcoxon rank regression [Cheng and Zhao, 2019, Li et al., 2016], computing the jackknife empirical likelihood (JEL) requires evaluating pairwise residual differences and incurs O(n2) operations. Repeating such evaluations, for example within a Metropolis-Hastings (MH) sampler, quickly becomes prohibitive. The second is the moment-evaluation barrier . In simulation-based, indirect-inference, and latent-variable problems, the moment function may itself be defined through an expectation [Carrasco and Florens, 2002], g(x, θ ) = EZ∼μz [h(x, Z, θ )] , and therefore may be unavailable in closed form. In such cases, direct full-data Bayesian moment-condition inference can be infeasible. Subsampling is a natural way to reduce computation, since mini-batches can replace repeated full-data evaluations. However, standard subsampling MCMC methods are mainly designed for likelihood-based posteriors and rely on the log-additive decomposition 

log p(X(n)|θ) = Pni=1 log p(Xi|θ), which permits low-variance mini-batch approximations to log-likelihoods. Moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihoods, such as EL and ETEL, generally lack this observation-level log-additive structure, because observations are coupled through empirical moment constraints. The central question, therefore, is how to 2construct a subsampled pseudo-posterior that approximates the inferential behavior of the intended full-data pseudo-posterior while replacing full-data evaluations with cheaper subsample-based evaluations. 

1.1 Our Approach and Contributions 

In this work, we develop a new family of subsampled pseudo-posteriors for Bayesian moment-condition inference. The framework replaces the full-data pseudo-likelihood with mini-batch surrogates and aggregates the resulting mini-batch targets into a subsampled pseudo-posterior. This target can be sampled using a subsampling MH algorithm, which greatly reduces the cost relative to MH sampling from the full-data pseudo-posterior, while preserving the inferential behavior of the full-data target through careful surrogate design. The central methodological challenge is to construct mini-batch approximations that retain the statistical behavior of the full-data pseudo-posterior. We introduce two com-plementary constructions. The first is a naive mini-batch surrogate, which treats each mini-batch as if it were the full dataset. We show that, with suitable tempering, the resulting subsampled pseudo-posterior can yield accurate point estimates. However, it also reveals a distortion specific to moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihoods. Each mini-batch has its own empirical moment equation and hence its own pseudo-likelihood center. Aggregating over mini-batches therefore produces a mixture of mini-batch pseudo-posteriors with randomly shifted centers. As a result, the aggregate can be correctly centered but overly diffuse. To correct this distortion, we introduce a moment-function-level control variate that aligns the mini-batch moment equations around a reference point, thereby removing the random center shift. Because the correction is applied at the moment-function level, it is broadly compatible with different pseudo-likelihoods, including EL, ETEL, and GMM-type criteria. Theoretically, we provide a detailed analysis for Bayesian ETEL. We establish explicit finite-sample total variation bounds for both the naive and control-variate constructions. The analysis allows over-identified moment restrictions and discontinuous moment func-tions, and extends to settings where the moment function is defined through an intractable expectation. Although the formal theory focuses on Bayesian ETEL, the proposed method-ology is more general because the construction acts at the moment-function level. We demonstrate this broader applicability through numerical studies involving Bayesian EL, Bayesian JEL, Bayesian ETEL with intractable moments, and MMD-Bayes. 31.2 Related Work 

This paper develops subsampled pseudo-posteriors for Bayesian inference based on moment conditions. It builds on the literature on Bayesian pseudo-inference, where the likelihood is replaced by a criterion induced by estimating equations. Examples include Bayesian EL [Lazar, 2003, Rao and Wu, 2010, Zhao et al., 2020], Bayesian ETEL [Schennach, 2005, Chib et al., 2018], Bayesian JEL [Jing et al., 2009, Li et al., 2016, Cheng and Zhao, 2019], and Bayesian GMM [Yin, 2009]; see Section 2.1 for a more detailed introduction. There is also a growing literature on computation for EL and related Bayesian pro-cedures. For Bayesian EL sampling, Chaudhuri et al. [2017] develop a Metropolized Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) approach, while Yu and Bondell [2024] propose varia-tional Bayes methods for approximate computation. Importance-sampling approaches have also been studied [Chang et al., 2025, Mengersen et al., 2013]. These methods address important computational issues for Bayesian EL sampling, whereas our work focuses on the complementary problem of using subsampling for scalable inference. For large-scale EL computation, Jaeger and Lazar [2020] propose a divide-and-conquer approximation based on local empirical likelihood contributions, and Liu and Li [2023] develop distributed strategies that aggregate EL- and ETEL-type estimators computed on subsamples. These methods primarily target frequentist point estimation, whereas our focus is Bayesian uncertainty quantification. Our work is also related to scalable Bayesian computation based on data subsam-pling. Many subsampling MCMC methods exploit the additive log-likelihood structure of Bayesian posteriors [Andrieu and Roberts, 2009, Quiroz et al., 2019, 2018, Wu et al., 2022, Welling and Teh, 2011, Nemeth and Fearnhead, 2021, Alquier et al., 2016, Zhang et al., 2020]. However, moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihoods, such as EL and ETEL, generally lack an observation-level log-additive decomposition. Thus, standard likelihood-subsampling arguments cannot be applied directly, which motivates our moment-function-level surrogate construction. 

1.3 Notation 

Throughout, n denotes the full sample size, m denotes the mini-batch size, and k denotes the number of latent draws for intractable moments. Let X(n) = ( X1, X 2, · · · , X n) ∈X n be the full data. We write (X(n))m for the set of all m-tuples ( eX1, . . . , eXm) with 

eXj ∈ { X1, . . . , X n}. We use ≲ and ≳ for inequalities up to a multiplicative constant independent of (m, n, k ). We use G#μ to denote the push-forward measure (if Z ∼ μ,then G(Z) ∼ G#μ). We write Cov( μ) for the covariance matrix of distribution μ. We use 

a∧b = min {a, b } and a∨b = max {a, b }. We write TV( ·, ·) for total variation distance, and 

∥ · ∥ p for the vector ℓp norm. We write N (μ, Σ) for the Gaussian distribution. For a map 

(x, θ ) 7 → g(x, θ ), let Jθg(x, θ ∗) be the Jacobian of θ 7 → g(x, θ ) at θ∗. ∥·∥ F denotes the matrix 4Frobenius norm. For symmetric matrices A and B, A ≽ B means that A − B is positive semidefinite. We use standard asymptotic notation with constants independent of (m, n, k ):

f = O(g) if f ≤ Cg , f = Ω( g) if f ≥ Cg , f = Θ( g) if C1g ≤ f ≤ C2g and f = o(g) if 

lim m,n,k →∞ f /g = 0 . We use ˜O(·), ˜Ω( ·), ˜Θ( ·) and ˜o(·) to suppress polylogarithmic factors in m, n, k . For example, f = ˜O(g) if f = O g · (log m)a(log n)b(log k)c for some constants 

a, b, c .

1.4 Organization 

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 reviews full-data moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors and the generic subsampled pseudo-posterior framework. Sections 3 and 4 develop mini-batch surrogate constructions for tractable and intractable moment functions. Section 5 discusses computational aspects. Section 6 establishes finite-sample total variation bounds for Bayesian ETEL in both tractable- and intractable-moment settings. Section 7 presents numerical results. Section 8 concludes with a discussion. Additional proofs and implementation details are provided in the Appendix. 

# 2 Full-data and Subsampled Pseudo-posteriors 

This section introduces the inferential targets used throughout the paper. We first review full-data Bayesian pseudo-posteriors induced by moment conditions. We then define the generic subsampled pseudo-posterior obtained by aggregating mini-batch pseudo-posteriors. 

2.1 Full-data Moment-condition-based Pseudo-posterior 

Moment-condition models specify parameters through estimating equations rather than a fully specified parametric likelihood. Let X(n) = ( X1, X 2, · · · , X n) be i.i.d. observations from an unknown population P∗. We assume the parameter θ ∈ Θ ⊂ Rd is identified by the moment restriction EX∼P ∗ [g(X, θ )] = 0 , where g : X × Θ → Rp is a moment function. Here θ need not fully parametrize P∗; instead, it may represent a functional θ(P∗) of 

P∗, such as a mean, a quantile, or a regression coefficient defined through orthogonality conditions. This formulation covers a wide range of problems in statistics. For example, in quantile regression at level τ ∈ (0 , 1) , a common choice is g(X = ( Y, W ), θ ) = W (1(Y −

W T θ < 0) − τ ). To perform Bayesian-style inference under such partial specification, a common approach is to replace the likelihood with a moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihood L(X(n), θ ), yielding the pseudo-posterior πn(θ|X(n)) ∝ π(θ)L(X(n), θ ), where 

π(θ) is the prior and L(X(n), θ ) is built to reward values of θ that make the sample moments n−1 Pni=1 g(Xi, θ ) close to zero. A common choice uses a GMM-type quadratic 5criterion [Yin, 2009, Chernozhukov and Hong, 2003], where the pseudo-likelihood is

Bayesian empirical likelihood (EL) [Lazar, 2003, Owen, 2001] and exponentially tilted empirical likelihood (ETEL) [Schennach, 2007, 2005] instead use likelihood-like objectives induced by the moment restrictions. Both EL and ETEL can be written in terms of implied probabilities {p(Xi, θ )}ni=1 assigned to the observed sample points. For each θ ∈ Θ, these probabilities are chosen to satisfy the sample moment constraints and to remain close to the uniform weight (n−1, . . . , n −1), with closeness measured by a “forward” or “backward” KL divergence [Wu and Lu, 2016, Schennach, 2007]. Given the sample X(n), the resulting pseudo-likelihood is L(X(n), θ ) = Qni=1 p(Xi, θ ), where  p(X1, θ ), p (X2, θ ), . . . , p (Xn, θ ) is obtained by solving

Pni=1 g(Xi, θ )= 0 . Thus, using them as working likelihoods yields pseudo-posteriors that concentrate on values of θ for which the sample moments are approximately satisfied. Moreover, under suitable regularity conditions, EL/ETEL pseudo-posteriors provide frequentist-calibrated uncertainty quantification, with coverage approaching the nominal level as n → ∞ [Fang and Mukerjee, 2006, Sueishi, 2024, Chib et al., 2018]. Several extensions of EL-type methods have been proposed. Adjusted empirical likelihood (AEL) [Chen et al., 2008] closely approximates EL while mitigating its bounded-support problem, and its Bayesian version is used in Yu and Bondell [2024] to address related computational issues. Bayesian penalized empirical likelihood [Chang et al., 2025] is designed to handle a growing number of moment conditions. Beyond standard moment-condition models, Li et al. [2016], Jing et al. [2009] propose jackknife empirical likelihood (JEL) procedures for U-statistic-type moments, with a Bayesian version developed by Cheng and Zhao [2019]. Despite their broad applicability, moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors are often computationally demanding. Their pseudo-likelihoods may involve complex quadratic forms, as in GMM, or inner constrained optimization problems, as in EL methods, making repeated density evaluations costly for large-scale data. The difficulty is greater when 6the moment function is simulation-based and cannot be evaluated exactly. Subsampled pseudo-posteriors provide a way to reduce these costs, and we introduce the generic construction next. 

2.2 Generic Subsampled Pseudo-posteriors 

Let eX(m) = ( eX1, . . . , eXm) denote a mini-batch drawn from the full data according to a sampling law μmn . When the moment function is intractable and has the form g(x, θ ) = 

EZ∼μz [h(x, Z, θ )] , let eZ(k) = ( Z1, . . . , Z k) ∈ Z k denote an auxiliary sample drawn i.i.d. from μz . Given a mini-batch surrogate L † : X m × Z k × Θ → R for the per-sample log pseudo-likelihood n−1 log L(X(n), θ ), and a scaling factor α > 0, we define

This type of subsampled pseudo-posterior also appears in the pseudo-marginal Metropolis–Hastings literature [Andrieu and Roberts, 2009], where it is typically viewed from a computational perspective as the marginal stationary distribution of an augmented MH chain. In particular, it can be sampled by running an MH algorithm on the augmented space with stationary distribution 

eπjoint  

> L†,α

, and then retaining only the θ-component. Computationally, each MH iteration evaluates the surrogate only on a mini-batch of size m, and, in the intractable-moment case, on k auxiliary draws, rather than evaluating the full-data pseudo-likelihood. This reduces the per-iteration cost in large-sample settings, especially for tall data, and makes pseudo-posterior inference feasible when the moment function is not available in closed form. The scaling factor α may induce a tempering effect [Alquier and Ridgway, 2020]. If 

L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ) were a sufficiently small-variance approximation to 1 

> n

log L(X(n), θ ), then choosing α = n would recover the scale of the untempered full-data pseudo-posterior. In general, however, eπL †,α (θ | X(n)) does not equal the tempered full-data target πα(θ |

X(n)) ∝ π(θ) exp( αn log L(X(n), θ )) , and the choice of α should account for the variability of 

L † across mini-batches. Thus, the statistical accuracy of the subsampled pseudo-posterior depends on both the surrogate construction and the scaling of α. The remainder of the paper develops surrogate constructions tailored to moment-condition-based pseudo-likelihoods and then describes implementation details for sampling from the subsampled pseudo-posteriors. 7Having introduced the generic subsampled pseudo-posterior, we now illustrate why the choice of mini-batch surrogate L is nontrivial. Indeed, the same subsampling template can behave quite differently for standard likelihood-based posteriors and moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors. For illustration, consider a toy model with data 

{Xi = ( Wi, Y i)}ni=1 , where Yi = Wiθ∗ + ϵi, ϵi ∼ N (0 , 1) , and θ∗ = 1 . We compare three targets: (i) the exact Bayesian posterior under the true likelihood, and (ii)–(iii) two moment-based pseudo-posteriors built from g(X, θ ) = ( Y − W θ )W , namely a Bayesian GMM with identity weighting matrix and a Bayesian ETEL. All three full-data posteriors share the same asymptotic limit N (1 , 1/n ), yet their subsampled version differs markedly under a naive mini-batch surrogate that treats each mini-batch as if it were the full sample (see Figure 1). For the standard posterior, the subsampled target is highly sensitive to the scaling parameter α: with n = 3000 and m = 300 , even α = 100 yields a distorted sampled density. In contrast, the two moment-based pseudo-posteriors are much less sensitive to α, producing stable samples even at α = n. This difference reflects the different variability of the naive mini-batch surrogate in the two settings. The GMM and log-ETEL objectives are, or can be well approximated up to an additive constant by, quadratic forms in the empirical moments n−1 Pni=1 g(Xi, θ ). Their mini-batch versions therefore tend to fluctuate less across subsamples than additive log-likelihood approximations. However, Figure 1 also shows that the moment-based subsampled targets are still overly diffuse relative to the intended full-data target, even with α = n. Thus, although naive mini-batching is more stable for moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors than for standard likelihood-based posteriors, it does not fully recover the correct posterior shape. This motivates the specialized moment-level surrogate constructions developed later.

This section constructs the mini-batch surrogates L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ) = L ( eX(m), θ ) that define the subsampled pseudo-posteriors in (2) when the moment function g is tractable. We introduce two constructions. The first is a naive surrogate that treats each mini-batch as if it were the full dataset. Despite its simplicity, with a suitable scaling parameter α,it can yield accurate point estimation. We then develop a variance-reduced surrogate that uses information from a reference estimator. This construction reduces mini-batch-induced variability and better recovers the full-data pseudo-posterior shape for uncertainty quantification. Both constructions fit within a unified framework. Given data X(n)

and a moment function g : X × Θ → Rp, we write the associated pseudo-likelihood as 

L(X(n), θ ; g) to make its dependence on g explicit. This notation covers GMM-type as well as EL/ETEL/AEL-type pseudo-likelihoods. We construct a surrogate by introducing a perturbed moment function eg : X × Θ × (X(n))m → Rp with eg(X, θ | eX(m)) ≈ g(X, θ ),and define the mini-batch surrogate as the per-sample log pseudo-likelihood induced by eg

on the mini-batch:

3.1 Naive Surrogate 

In the naive construction, we set eg(·, · | eX(m)) ≡ g in (3). The resulting mini-batch surrogate is denoted by Lnaive ( eX(m), θ ) to distinguish it from the variance-reduced con-structions introduced next. As Figure 1 shows, for a range of α, the posterior samples based on Lnaive are centered near the target posterior mean, suggesting that the corre-sponding subsampled pseudo-posterior eπLnaive ,α (θ|X(n)) can yield good point estimates. However, the same figure also shows that the sampled densities are substantially more dispersed than the full-data pseudo-posterior πn(θ|X(n)), and this over-dispersion persists even when α = n. This occurs because, for each fixed mini-batch eX(m), the mini-batch pseudo-likelihood and its induced pseudo-posterior are centered near the θ value that best satisfies its own sample moment equation, m−1 Pmj=1 g( eXj , θ ) = 0 . This center varies across mini-batches. The subsampled pseudo-posterior hence aggregates over many component posteriors with different centers. This aggregation preserves the full-data center reasonably well but inflates the dispersion. Figure 2a illustrates this behavior. We can see the component pseudo-posteriors (black curves) for different mini-batches 

eX(m) have comparable widths but are shifted relative to one another, producing an overly diffuse subsampled pseudo-posterior (red curve). This observation motivates a practical scaling choice and a post hoc correction. 3.2 Variance-reduced Surrogate With Zero-order Control Variate 

The limitations of the naive surrogate motivate a more refined construction. While the naive construction can locate the center of the resulting subsampled pseudo-posterior reasonably well, it can also induce an inflated dispersion. This observation suggests a natural remedy in which we treat the point estimator as a reliable anchor, and then use it to construct a variance-reduced surrogate. g(Xi, θ †),

11 which is a standard control variate pattern [Baker et al., 2019, Bardenet et al., 2017]. The first two terms m−1 Pmj=1 g( eXj , θ ) and m−1 Pmj=1 g( eXj , θ †) are computed from the same subset, so the fluctuations from mini-batch sampling are strongly correlated and largely cancel when θ ≈ θ†. Therefore, the mini-batch sample moment from eg provides a variance-reduced estimator of gn(θ). Since no gradient information is used in this construction, we refer to it as a zero-order control variate and denote the associated mini-batch surrogate by Lzcv .

2. Recentering interpretation. Under the naive surrogate, each mini-batch pseudo-likelihood is centered near the θ value that best satisfies its own sample moment m−1 Pmj=1 g( eXj , θ ) = 0. These centers vary across mini-batches, producing the over-dispersion described be-fore. The correction in (4) reduces this variation by enforcing 1

> m

Pmj=1 eg( eXj , θ † | eX(m)) = 

> 1
> n

Pni=1 g(Xi, θ †) for every eX(m). For example, if n−1 Pni=1 g(Xi, θ †) = 0 , then all mini-batch pseudo-likelihoods are centered at θ†. More generally, when θ† is close to the full-data pseudo-posterior center, the mini-batch pseudo-likelihoods are approximately aligned in the same region of the parameter space. This alignment reduces the random center shifts that make the naive subsampled pseudo-posterior overly diffuse. As shown in Figure 2b, the control-variate surrogate closely aligns the component pseudo-posteriors and yields a sharper subsampled pseudo-posterior than the naive construction. Table 1 (“ZCV” column) illustrates the performance of this variance-reduced construc-tion. With the zero-order control variate, even when m ≪ n, the zero-order control variate yields coverage, interval length, and ESS close to those of the full-data target. Thus, the subsampled pseudo-posterior preserves the inferential behavior of the full-data pseudo-posterior without substantially degrading Markov-chain mixing. Since each subsampling MH iteration evaluates only m observations rather than all n, this comparable accuracy and mixing is achieved at substantially lower per-iteration cost. 

3.3 Higher-order Control Variates 

The zero-order control variate (ZCV) in (4) is gradient-free, easy to implement, and applicable even when the moment function is discontinuous. When g is smooth in θ,however, we can further reduce mini-batch variability by using derivative information. In particular, using the Jacobian of g, we define a first-order control-variate (FCV) perturbed moment function: 

egfcv (x, θ | eX(m)) = egzcv (x, θ | eX(m)) − 1

m

Jθg( eXj , θ †)( θ − θ†) + 1

X

Jθg(Xi, θ †)( θ − θ†),

where egzcv (x, θ | eX(m)) is the zero-order construction in (4) and Jθg(Xi, θ †) denotes the Jacobian of g(Xi, ·) at θ†. Compared with ZCV, FCV corrects the mini-batch sample moment by matching not only the value at θ†, but also the local linear behavior, so mini-12 batch fluctuations are further reduced when θ ≈ θ†. Plugging egfcv into (3) then yields the corresponding first-order control variate mini-batch surrogate, denoted by Lfcv ( eX(m), θ ).While this construction requires gradients, in return it provides stronger variance control and can tolerate a less precise reference point. A formal Bayesian ETEL analysis is given in Theorem 5 of Appendix A. Thus, the practical choice between ZCV and FCV depends on whether Jacobians are available and inexpensive to compute. Figure 3 compares ZCV and FCV for Bayesian EL Huber regression. Overall, for a fixed mini-batch size, FCV converges faster than ZCV and is more robust to perturbations of the reference point. Finally, even when g is discontinuous, one may still use the first-order control variate by replacing Jθg with the Jacobian of a smooth surrogate (e.g., a sigmoid approximation to an indicator). Since the smoothing is used only to construct the control variate and the zero-order term already provides the baseline correction, the method is relatively insensitive to the particular smoothing choice. Figure 3b shows how the SW 2 distance between the subsampled and benchmark posteriors varies with the perturbation scale η, averaged over 100 replications. 13 4 Mini-batch Surrogate Constructions: Intractable Case 

In many applications, the moment function g : X × Θ → Rp is unavailable in closed form. Instead, one observes a simulable representation g(X, θ ) = EZ∼μz [h(X, Z, θ )] , for a known distribution μz and a tractable function h. This setting arises naturally in simulation-based and indirect-inference problems [Carrasco and Florens, 2002, Frazier et al., 2023, Pakes and Pollard, 1989], where the structural model is easy to simulate but its implied moments are intractable, as well as in latent-variable models, where g requires integrating over unobserved variables [Schennach, 2014]. Repeatedly approximating g(Xi, θ ) for every observation and proposed θ can make standard Bayesian pseudo-inference impractical. A common remedy [Carrasco and Florens, 2002] replaces g(x, θ ) by a Monte Carlo average based on a fixed k draws from μz , but achieving sufficient precision may require very large 

k, often far exceeding n. In contrast, the subsampled pseudo-posterior can use smaller k,since the latent draws are refreshed through the outer aggregation. Specifically, a naive surrogate is

α ≍ m ∧ k; see Theorem 3 and Figure 4 for theoretical and numerical justifications. As in the tractable case, let θ† = θ†(X(n)) be a reference point. Define the zero-order control-variate surrogate

Theoretical support for Bayesian ETEL in this setting is given in Theorem 4 of Section 6.2. A first-order control variate can also be constructed by extending the tractable-moment ideas; see Appendix A for further details. Finally, the approach may also be extended to conditional latent-variable models of the form g(X, θ ) = EZ∼μz|X [h(X, Z, θ )] , where for each mini-batch datapoint eXj , we generate conditional latent draws eZ(k)

In this section, we discuss sampling procedures for the proposed subsampled pseudo-posteriors and summarize practical computational considerations. 

5.1 Sampling from the Subsampled Pseudo-posteriors 

We focus on the subsampling MH (SMH) algorithm for posterior sampling as it is simple and broadly applicable. For generality, we present the case where g is intractable. The tractable case is recovered by omitting the Z-sampling step and taking L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ) = 

L ( eX(m), θ ). The algorithm is a pseudo-marginal MH scheme [Andrieu and Roberts, 2009] that augments the state with a mini-batch and, when needed, auxiliary latent draws, and refreshes them during the Markov transition. Relative to likelihood-based subsampling MH [Wu et al., 2022], the key difference is that L † may be non-additive and built from a moment-level control variate. We also use a laziness parameter ζ ∈ [0 , 1) , which controls how often a new mini-batch and auxiliary sample are proposed. Algorithm 1 summarizes the procedure. 

Algorithm 1: Subsampling Metropolis-Hastings (SMH) Algorithm 1. Choose a surrogate L †, batch sizes m, k , a scaling factor α, a proposal distribution 

q(· | θ, eX(m), eZ(k)), and a laziness parameter ζ. For control-variate surrogates, choose a reference point θ† and compute/pre-estimate the required full-data quantities at θ†.2. Initialize at θ0 and draw ( eX(m)0 , eZ(k)0 ) ∼ μmn × μ⊗kz .3. At iteration l ≥ 1, propose θ ∼ q(· | θl−1, eX(m)

,whose θ-marginal is the desired subsampled pseudo-posterior. In practice, we use the naive surrogate with α = m∧k mainly for point estimation and reference-point construction, and the control-variate surrogates with α = n to approximate the untempered full-data pseudo-posterior. The laziness parameter ζ ∈ [0 , 1) does not change the stationary distribution, 15 but ζ > 0 can help improve mixing of the Markov chain. Intuitively, if the chain reaches a state (θ, eX(m), eZ(k)) where L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ) is unusually large, while L †( eX(m)′, eZ(k)′, θ )

is typically much smaller for most newly sampled ( eX(m)′, eZ(k)′), the chain may stagnate while waiting for mini-batches similar to ( eX(m), eZ(k)). A nonzero laziness parameter, set to 

ζ = 0 .5 by default in our experiments, mitigates this issue without altering the stationary distribution, and can markedly improve mixing (see Figure 5 for an illustration). Note that SMH is not the only possible sampler. One could instead use importance sampling on the augmented space (θ, eX(m), eZ(k)), drawing θ from an importance proposal r(·)

and ( eX(m), eZ(k)) from μmn × μ⊗kz , then reweighting by π(θ) exp( α · L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ )) /r (θ).This is highly parallelizable because the weights are computed independently, but its performance depends on choosing an importance proposal close to the target. Adaptive importance-sampling strategies, such as the recycling successive sampling procedure of Chang et al. [2025], could therefore provide a useful alternative implementation. 

5.2 Computational Considerations 

1. Choice of reference point. The reference point can be obtained in several ways. A simple option is to run a standard optimizer on the pseudo-likelihood and use the resulting estimate. Since an exact optimizer is not required, it is often enough to optimize a convenient surrogate. For example, in the Huber regression application, the OLS estimator can serve as a good reference point. Alternatively, one can estimate the mean of the naive subsampled pseudo-posterior with appropriate tempering (we take α = m ∧ k by default). Because this approach is sampling-based, it can be more robust when the pseudo-likelihood has multiple stationary points, where direct optimization may be sensitive to initialization. It also applies when g(X, θ ) is discontinuous in θ, where standard optimization can be difficult. 

2. Choice of proposal distributions. A common default is a random-walk proposal, but it can be inefficient in high dimensions. When the moment function g is nonsmooth in θ,a practical alternative is to run a short random-walk pilot chain, use the pilot draws to estimate the posterior mean and covariance, and then construct a Gaussian independent proposal from these estimates. When g is differentiable in θ, it is preferable to exploit gradient information. Gradient-based proposals, such as Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm (MALA) or HMC, can scale much better with dimension than random-walk proposals. In the SMH setting, these proposals can be built using stochastic gradient information, namely the gradient of the mini-batch surrogate objective. Table 2 illustrates that stochastic-gradient proposals can help the subsampling method scale to higher dimensions. 

3. Choice of the mini-batch size. The mini-batch size m (or k) is a key tuning parameter. It controls both the per-iteration cost and the discrepancy between the subsampled and 16 full-data pseudo-posteriors. Since this discrepancy is difficult to evaluate directly, we use the effective sample size (ESS) from a fixed number of SMH draws as a practical diagnostic. ESS measures the number of effectively independent draws in a correlated MCMC output and a smaller ESS indicates stronger autocorrelation and poorer mixing. When the mini-batch surrogate is too noisy, SMH accepts or rejects proposals using a high-variance approximation, which degrades mixing and lowers ESS. Conversely, an ESS close to that of full-data MH suggests that the surrogate variability is sufficiently controlled, so that SMH behaves similarly to the full-data chain. In our experiments, ESS is also strongly correlated with inferential accuracy; see Table 2. Values of m that produce inaccurate inference have much smaller ESS than larger choices of m. This motivates a simple pilot rule: run SMH for a short pilot phase and gradually increase m until the ESS shows no substantial improvement. Table 2: We revisit the Bayesian EL Huber regression in Figure 3 with d = 20 . We use a MALA proposal for full-data MH, and a stochastic-gradient MALA proposal for SMH, with the same covariance matrix. Over 1000 replications (each with 10 ,000 MH draws), we report empirical 95% CI coverage and average interval length ( ×10 −2) for θ1, as well as ESS averaged across coordinates. Subscripts 0 and 1 denote zero- and first-order control variates; “Full” denotes full-data MH. We can see ESS closely tracks inferential accuracy. For example, increasing m from 200 to 500 substantially shortens the credible intervals and brings coverage closer to the nominal 95% level; these improvements coincide with a marked increase in ESS. Similar patterns hold for other dimensions, with results for 

d ∈ { 5, 10 , 40 } reported in Appendix B.1. 

m 200 500 800 1000 1500 2000 Full ( n = 20 , 000 )Coverage 0 97.6 96.0 95.5 95.0 95.1 94.5 94.6 Coverage 1 95.4 94.9 94.5 94.8 94.6 94.4 Length 0 3.48 3.11 3.02 3.00 2.97 2.95 2.91 Length 1 3.04 2.96 2.93 2.93 2.92 2.92 ESS 0 313 774 1044 1165 1364 1485 1960 ESS 1 447 984 1239 1344 1518 1611 

# 6 Theoretical Results and Their Consequences 

This section studies the statistical accuracy of the proposed subsampled pseudo-posteriors. Throughout, we focus on the Bayesian ETEL posterior and, for simplicity, assume that each mini-batch is drawn with replacement from the empirical measure μn of X(n), so that μmn = μ⊗mn . The results quantify the total variation distance between the subsampled pseudo-posterior and the corresponding full-data pseudo-posterior. We first analyze the case where the moment function can be evaluated exactly, and then extend the bounds to intractable moment functions defined through auxiliary simulation. Note that under suitable regularity conditions, mini-batch surrogates based on (optimally 17 weighted) GMM and EL-type pseudo-likelihoods are asymptotically equivalent to ETEL surrogate [Schennach, 2007, Newey and Smith, 2004]. We therefore expect analogous results to hold for these pseudo-posteriors. 

6.1 Analysis of Tractable Moment Function 

We begin with the case where the moment function g is tractable to evaluate. Since non-smooth moments are common in applications (e.g., moments involving indicator functions), we adopt the framework of Tang and Yang [2022], which does not require pointwise smoothness of the map θ 7 → g(x, θ ) for every x. Instead, it assumes smoothness of the population moment map G(θ) := EX∼P ∗ [g(X, θ )] ∈ Rp, which is often natural since the expectation operator has a smoothing effect. For example, EX∼P ∗ [1(X ≤ θ)] becomes the cumulative distribution function of X evaluated at θ. We then impose the following assumptions. 

Assumption 1. (Population moment map and prior). Assume 1. (Identification and parameter space). The parameter space Θ ⊂ Rd is compact, and 

θ∗ ∈ Θ◦ is the unique solution of G(θ) = 0 over Θ.2. (Smoothness of the population moment map). G is twice continuously differentiable on Θ and all mixed partial derivatives up to order two are uniformly bounded on Θ.3. (Non-degeneracy at θ∗). Let Hθ := JθG(θ) ∈ Rp×d denote the Jacobian of G at θ

and let ∆θ := EX∼P ∗

b > 0 such that ∆θ∗ ⪰ aIp and H⊤ 

> θ∗

Hθ∗ ⪰ bId. Moreover, θ 7 → ∆θ is locally Lipschitz around θ∗, i.e., there exist positive constants r, L such that for all θ ∈ Θ with 

∥θ − θ∗∥2 ≤ r, ∥∆θ − ∆θ∗ ∥F ≤ L∥θ − θ∗∥2.4. (Local regularity of the prior). Let π denote the prior density. There exist positive constants (r, L, C ), such that π(θ∗) ≥ C, and ∀ θ ∈ Θ with ∥θ − θ∗∥2 ≤ r, |π(θ) −

π(θ∗)| ≤ L∥θ − θ∗∥2.

Assumption 1 ensures that the population moment map is well-behaved. Since 

g(X, θ ) may be discontinuous in θ, the key technical step is to relate the behavior of the empirical moments n−1 Pni=1 g(Xi, θ ) to their population counterpart G(θ) uniformly over 

θ ∈ Θ. To enable such uniform control, we impose the following conditions on the class 

{g(·, θ ) : θ ∈ Θ}.

Remark 1. The compactness of Θ and the uniform boundedness of g are imposed mainly for technical convenience, as they simplify the derivation of uniform concentration bounds. If g(x, θ ) is uniformly twice differentiable in θ with bounded derivatives, then Assumption 2 holds with β = 1 . Assumption 2 also allows for discontinuous moment functions, as the smoothness index β is defined at the population level (after taking expectation over X). For instance, Tang and Yang [2022] verifies Assumptions 1 and 2 with β = 12 for quantile regression and for SVM, where g is discontinuous in θ in both settings.

The definition of the additional weighting factor ω( eX(m)) is given in Appendix C.2.2. 

Remark 2. Theorem 1 formalizes the shifted-mixture picture. In the exactly identified case 

d = p, it admits a particularly simple interpretation. The naive subsampled Bayesian ETEL 

eπLnaive ,α (θ | X(n)) is close to a tempered full-data Bayesian ETEL πα(θ | X(n)) convolved with an additional zero-mean random shift ε( eX(m)) = bθ( eX(m)) − bθ(X(n)). This shift 

ε( eX(m)) arises because θ 7 → L ( eX(m), θ ) has a mini-batch-dependent maximizer. It can be interpreted as the gap between the mini-batch surrogate maximizer (approximated by 

bθ( eX(m))) and the full-data pseudo-likelihood maximizer (approximated by bθ(X(n))), with 

19 variability controlled by m. This yields two regimes. (1) If α ≪ m, the random shift 

ε( eX(m)) is negligible relative to the spread of πα(θ|X(n)), and eπLnaive ,α (θ|X(n)) closely matches the tempered target. (2) If α ≫ m, the random shift dominates the variability, leading to inflated credible intervals even when α is large. This explains the empirical pattern in Figure 1 that once α > m , further increasing α has little effect on tightening the pseudo-posterior shape. A useful consequence, however, is that the added shift is zero-mean, so point estimation remains reliable. Furthermore, by the central limit theorem, conditional on X(n), ε( eX(m)) is asymptotically Gaussian distributed with mean 0 and covariance m−1H−1 

> θ∗

∆θ∗ (H⊤ 

> θ∗

)−1. Because this “sandwich” form, up to scaling, matches the asymptotic covariance of the Bayesian ETEL posterior [Chib et al., 2018], one can recover the target covariance by an appropriate rescaling. 

Remark 3. In the over-identified case (d < p ) , the analysis is more involved because, for a given mini-batch eX(m), there is generally no θ satisfying 1

> m

Pmj=1 g( eXj , θ ) = 0 (whereas when d = p this root is approximated by bθ( eX(m))). Consequently, the maximum values of the mini-batch surrogate, i.e., max θ∈Θ L ( eX(m), θ ), vary across mini-batches, inducing the weight factor ω( eX(m)) in the limiting approximation. 

Despite the different limiting forms in the exactly-identified and over-identified case, when the moment condition is correctly specified, the corresponding subsampled Bayesian ETEL posteriors both have accurate centerings and, after suitable rescaling, the correct covariance structures, as summarized in the following Corollary 1. 

Corollary 1. Assume the corresponding assumptions in Theorem 1 hold for the cases of 

d = p and d < p . Then, R θ · eπLnaive ,α (θ | X(n)) d θ − R θ · πn(θ | X(n)) d θ 2 = eO(α− 1+ β

Remark 4. An important implication of Corollary 1 is that both the posterior mean and rescaled covariance errors are minimized by α = eΘ( m). Notably, the optimal polynomial scaling of α in m does not depend on the smoothness index β. With this choice, the point-estimation error is of order eO(m−(1+ β)/2), so m = eΩ n1/(1+ β) yields root-n accuracy. Corollary 1 also suggests that Bayesian ETEL, and more broadly moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors, can tolerate much larger values of α under naive subsampling than conventional log-additive posteriors. For example, in the likelihood-based setting, Wu et al. [2022] require α = eo(√m) for the subsampled posterior to yield a consistent point estimator, whereas in our setting, in particular when d = p, we allow α = eo(m1+ β/ 2).

Remark 5. Corollary 1 also shows the covariance of the naive subsampled Bayesian ETEL satisfies Cov( πLnaive ,α (θ|X(n))) ≈ ( nα + nm ) · Cov( πn(θ|X(n))) . The term nα reflects the usual tempering effect from the scaling factor α, while nm comes from the variance 

20 of the random shift ε( eX(m)) = bθ( eX(m)) − bθ(X(n)). Choosing α = m balances these two sources of variability. This covariance-rescaling argument also justifies the post hoc shape correction discussed in Section 3.1. However, it relies on the well-calibration of Bayesian ETEL, namely that its asymptotic covariance matches the sampling covariance of its center. If this calibration property fails, for example in Bayesian GMM with a misspecified weighting matrix, the covariance of the random center shift ε( eX(m)) may not match the covariance of the full-data pseudo-posterior. The simple rescaling may then fail to recover the full-data pseudo-posterior covariance. On the positive side, naive subsampling may thus provide a diagnostic for the calibration of moment-condition-based pseudo-posteriors: if the induced covariance structure is inconsistent with the full-data target, it may indicate poor calibration. 

We now analyze the zero-order control-variate surrogate L = Lzcv . To obtain sharper bounds in the smooth regime, we impose the following pointwise regularity condition. 

Assumption 3. (Pointwise smoothness). There exist constants L > 0 and β1 ∈ [0 , 1] so that for every x ∈ X and θ ∈ Θ, ∥g(x, θ ) − g(x, θ ∗)∥2 ≤ L∥θ − θ∗∥β1 

> 2

.

Note that β1 = 0 is allowed, in which case Assumption 3 follows immediately from the uniform boundedness of g. When β1 > 0, the additional pointwise smoothness can yield faster rates. Recall that θ† = θ†(X(n)) denotes the reference point and let Cm,n denote an upper bound on its deviation from the full-data pseudo-posterior mean. 

Theorem 2. (Zero-order control variate). Assume that Assumptions 1, 2 and 3 hold. Suppose there exists a positive constant γ so that m ≥ nγ . Then, there exists a set

Remark 6. Theorem 2 shows that the control-variate correction removes the distortion from naive subsampling. The condition m ≥ nγ is imposed for technical reasons, ensuring that m grows at least polynomially with n. To simplify the bound, suppose Cm,n = O(n−1/2).In the smooth case β = β1 = 1 , the error is eO(n−1/2 + m−1), so consistency holds for any polynomial mini-batch size m = nγ with γ > 0. In the discontinuous case, where the pointwise smoothness index is β1 = 0 , the error becomes eO(n−1/2 + n/m 2 + n1−β /m ).Consistency then requires m = nγ1 for some γ1 > max {1/2, 1 − β}, where β is the population smoothness index. Thus, discontinuous moments generally require larger mini-batches. 

6.2 Analysis of Intractable Moment Functions 

We now study the intractable setting, where the moment function is defined via an inner expectation, g(X, θ ) = EZ∼μz [h(X, Z, θ )] . As before, we focus on Bayesian ETEL and 21 assume mini-batches are sampled with replacement. For technical simplicity, we restrict attention here to the smooth regime and impose the following pointwise smoothness condition. 

Assumption 4. (Pointwise smoothness of the Z-dependent moment). The function 

h : X × Z × Θ → Rp is twice differentiable in θ, and all mixed partial derivatives with respect to θ up to order two are uniformly bounded over (x, z, θ ) ∈ X × Z × Θ.

Remark 7. For technical simplicity, we analyze the naive construction only in the exactly identified case ( d = p). As in the tractable setting, the naive surrogate gives accurate point estimation, with optimal scaling factor α = eΘ( m ∧ k) and point-estimation error

# 7 Numerical Studies 

In this section, we use numerical studies to validate the proposed subsampled pseudo-posteriors. We assess performance along two aspects: statistical accuracy and sampling efficiency . Statistical accuracy refers to the discrepancy between the subsampled pseudo-posterior and the corresponding full-data pseudo-posterior. Sampling efficiency measures whether the computational gain from cheaper mini-batch evaluations is offset by poorer Monte Carlo efficiency when the target is sampled by SMH algorithm. To assess statistical accuracy, we compare the inferential output of the subsampled pseudo-posterior with that of the full-data pseudo-posterior. In our experiments, both targets are sampled for a fixed number of iterations using the corresponding MH or SMH implementation. We construct credible intervals for the parameter of interest and report frequentist coverage probabilities together with average interval lengths. This evaluates whether the subsampled pseudo-posterior delivers uncertainty quantification comparable to the full-data pseudo-posterior. To assess sampling efficiency, we report the effective sample size (ESS), which estimates the number of effectively independent draws. All coverages and average interval lengths are computed over 1000 replications. Although the theory assumes mini-batches sampled with replacement from X(n) for technical convenience, the experiments use sampling without replacement to avoid duplicated observations within a mini-batch and improve numerical stability. Additional implementation details are provided in Appendix B. 

7.1 Bayesian Jackknife EL for Wilcoxon-rank Regression

23 Table 3: Bayesian JEL for Wilcoxon-rank regression. The table reports, for θ1, the coverage probability (%) of the 95% credible interval, the interval length ( ×10 −2), the ESS, and the average ESS generated per second (ESS/s). Throughout, runtimes are measured in Python on an Intel Xeon Gold 6348 CPU. Subscripts 0 and 1 denote the zero-and first-order control variates. 

m Coverage 0 Coverage 1 Length 0 Length 1 ESS 0 ESS 1 ESS 0/s ESS 1/s 200 95.9 94.7 15.54 14.87 1190 1245 83.54 44.71 250 95.0 94.4 15.22 14.73 1234 1272 67.13 32.93 300 94.6 94.2 15.03 14.62 1253 1294 52.98 24.80 Full 94.1 14.45 1316 15.37 We set the sample size to n = 1000 and the parameter dimension to d = 2 . Data-generation details are given in Appendix B.2. The full-data target is the Bayesian jackknife empirical likelihood (JEL) posterior [Cheng and Zhao, 2019]. Sampling from full-data Bayesian JEL is challenging because the moment function is discontinuous, precluding direct use of standard gradient-based samplers. Moreover, the JEL objective depends on all pairwise residual comparisons, incurring an O(n2) per-iteration cost. Our subsampled pseudo-posterior framework extends directly to this setting by replacing the empirical means in the moment-function surrogate, eg, with the corresponding U -statistics; see Appendix B.2 for details. We take the OLS estimator as the reference point θ† and use the same random-walk proposal across methods. To build a first-order control variate, we smooth the discontinuous sign function with a sigmoid approximation to obtain the gradients used in the surrogate construction. Note that the smoothed function is used 

only to compute the gradient term in the first-order control variate; our target remains the Bayesian JEL posterior defined with the original (discontinuous) g. Consequently, the method is insensitive to the particular smoothing choice (see Figure 8 in Appendix B.2 for an illustration). We consider mini-batch sizes m ∈ { 200 , 250 , 300 }. Table 3 reports empirical coverage, 95% credible interval length, ESS, and ESS generated per second for the first component of θ. Across all settings, SMH yields ESS values comparable to the full-data algorithm while reducing the per-iteration cost from O(n2) to O(m2). In terms of inference, the subsampled pseudo-posterior is slightly more conservative, producing longer credible intervals, but this can mitigate under-coverage when the full-data pseudo-posterior understates uncertainty. 

7.2 Bayesian ETEL for g-and-k Inference on Real Data 

We consider a generative family {μθ}θ∈Θ defined by μθ = G(·, θ )#μz , and aim to infer θ.In many such models, G is highly nonlinear and non-invertible, making the likelihood intractable and precluding classical Bayesian inference. Instead, parameter identification can be achieved via moment conditions, motivating moment-condition-based Bayesian 24 pseudo-inference. A key difficulty is that the resulting moment functions typically involve expectations with respect to the latent variable Z, which are rarely available in closed form. As a concrete example, consider the g-and-k distribution [Haynes et al., 1997, Prangle, 2017] with base μz = N (0 , 1) and generator G(z, θ ) = θ1 +θ2

h

1 + 0 .81−exp( −θ3z)1+exp( −θ3z)

i

(1+ z2)θ4 z, 

where θ = ( θ1, θ 2, θ 3, θ 4)⊤ controls location, scale, skewness, and kurtosis, respectively. We use p = 6 moments based on moment matching and quantile matching. Specifically, for 

i = 1 , 2, we set gi(x, θ ) = xi − EZ∼μz [G(Z, θ )i], and for i = 3 , . . . , 6, we use quantile-level moments gi(x, θ ) = 1(x ≤ G(qτi−2 , θ )) − τi−2, where qτ denotes the τ -quantile of N (0 , 1) 

and (τ1, τ 2, τ 3, τ 4) = (0 .3, 0.4, 0.6, 0.7) . This yields an over-identified setting with p > d = 4 .We use heavy-tailed financial data given by log-differences of the CAD/USD exchange rate from the Garch dataset, with n = 1866 . The full-data target is the Bayesian ETEL posterior. Here the moment map is discontinuous in θ due to the indicator moments and intractable due to the latent expectations. The moment condition is also likely misspecified, since the data are real and the model is over-identified. To obtain a full-data benchmark, we approximate μz by the empirical distribution of N = 5 × 10 6 draws from 

N (0 , 1) . For subsampled Bayesian ETEL, we vary the data and latent mini-batch sizes 

m and k. For sampling, full-data MH uses a random walk pilot run of 10 , 000 iterations to estimate the posterior mean and covariance, followed by 10 , 000 iterations with a Gaussian independence proposal with mean and covariance fixed to the pilot estimates. For SMH, we use a three-stage warm-up: (i) naive surrogate with a random-walk proposal (α = m ∧ k, 10 , 000 iterations) to obtain an initial reference point θ†. (ii) ZCV surrogate with a random-walk proposal ( α = n, 2, 000 iterations) to refine θ† and estimate the covariance. (iii) A final run ( α = n, 10 , 000 iterations) that uses θ† to build the zero-order control variate and a Gaussian independence proposal with mean and covariance set to the stage-(ii) estimates. Figure 4 compares marginal posterior densities for subsampled Bayesian ETEL and the full-data benchmark. Table 4 reports average ESS and ESS/s for both full-data MH and SMH, computed from the 10 ,000 final-stage MCMC iterations; the runtimes used for ESS/s include all algorithmic stages. The table also reports Mdiff and Qdiff . Here, 

Mdiff is the coordinate-averaged difference between subsampled and full-data posterior means, normalized by the corresponding full-data marginal posterior standard deviation. Similarly, Qdiff is the coordinate-averaged difference between subsampled and full-data 

95% credible-interval endpoints, normalized by the corresponding full-data interval length. To further assess inferential accuracy, we conduct a simulation study focusing on the empirical coverage of 95% credible intervals. We set the ground-truth parameter θ∗ to the Bayesian ETEL posterior mean estimated by full-data MH in the preceding real-data experiment. In each replication, we generate a synthetic dataset of size n = 1866 by drawing i.i.d. Zi ∼ N (0 , 1) and setting Xi = G(Zi, θ ∗). 7.3 MMD-Bayes Inference 

MMD-Bayes [Cherief-Abdellatif and Alquier, 2020] defines a pseudo-likelihood by mea-suring the discrepancy between the empirical distribution μn = n−1 Pni=1 δXi and the model distribution Pθ via the maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) induced by a ker-nel κ. Given Xn = ( X1, . . . , X n), the per-sample negative log pseudo-likelihood is 

MMD 2(μn, Pθ) = 1

> n2

Pni′=1 κ(Xi, X i′ ) − 2

> n

Pni=1 EY ∼P θ [κ(Xi, Y )] + EY,Y ′∼P θ [κ(Y, Y ′)] .26 This quantity can also be viewed as a GMM-type criterion. Indeed, assume the kernel κ

admits the factorization κ(x, y ) = R eκ(x, t ) eκ(y, t ) d t, and that Pθ is a generative model 

Pθ = G(·, θ )#μz . Then define ht(X, Z, θ ) = eκ(X, t ) − eκ(G(Z, θ ), t ), the empirical MMD can be represented as 

MMD 2(μn, Pθ) =

Thus the empirical MMD can be seen as a GMM-type objective with a continuum of intractable moment functions gt(X, θ ) indexed by t, where the usual finite sum over moments is replaced by an integration over t. The naive surrogate construction (combined with tempering) extends directly to this setting and provides a practical tool for point estimation. To illustrate, we consider a generative model with a discontinuous generative map G(z, θ ) = ( 12 1(z1 ≤ θ1) + θ1 + θ2 + z1, 12 1(z1 ≤ θ1) + θ2 + z2)T , latent distribution 

μz = N  (1 , 1) , ( 1 0.20.2 1 ), true parameter θ∗ = (1 , 1) and n = 2000 . Because G is discontin-uous, standard gradient-based MMD minimization is difficult, making sampling-based approaches particularly attractive. We therefore use the SMH algorithm to sample from the subsampled pseudo-posterior with the naive surrogate and tempering, and use the resulting draws to obtain a point estimate of θ. Specifically, given a data mini-batch eX(m)

and a latent mini-batch eZ(k), we approximate the negative empirical MMD using the mini-batch surrogate 

L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ) = −

where bμ( eX(m)) and bμ(eZ(k)) denote the empirical distributions of datasets eX(m) and eZ(k).The subsampled pseudo-posterior is then defined by eπL †,α (θ|X(n)) ∝ E( eX(m),eZ(k))∼μmn ×μ⊗kz [π(θ)exp( α · L †( eX(m), eZ(k), θ ))] . We consider multiple (m, k ) choices with scaling factor 

α = m ∧ k. Table 5 reports the mean absolute deviation (MAD) of the posterior mean from the true θ∗, ESS, and ESS/s, computed from 7000 SMH draws after 3000 

burn-in iterations and averaged over 100 replications. Across moderate-to-large batch sizes, the posterior-mean estimates are stable, with noticeable degradation only appearing for the smallest configuration m = k = 100 ; even there, the loss in accuracy remains modest. Moreover, aided by tempering, the ESS does not collapse as m and k decrease, indicating that SMH maintains reasonable mixing even under aggressive subsampling. Furthermore, the “laziness” parameter ζ, which controls how often mini-batches are refreshed, is crucial here. As shown in Figures 5 (a)–(b), even with m = n (i.e., using the full data for X), setting ζ = 0 (refreshing eZ(k) at every step) can cause the chain to become sticky, whereas 

ζ = 0 .5 largely mitigates this issue. In addition, Figures 5 (b)–(c) indicate that combining smaller mini-batches (m, k ) with tempering can be advantageous. It significantly reduces 27 the per-step cost and permits larger proposal step sizes, which in turn shortens the burn-in period. Extending the zero-order control variate to this setting is nontrivial because the moment function is infinite-dimensional, indexed by a continuum of t’s. We present one possible construction, together with numerical results, in Appendix B.4.

# 8 Discussion 

In this work, we develop subsampled pseudo-posteriors for scalable Bayesian moment-condition inference, with a subsampling MH implementation used to sample from the resulting targets. The proposed construction addresses two sources of computational difficulty, namely repeated full-data pseudo-likelihood evaluations when the sample size 

n is large and repeated evaluation of moment functions defined through intractable expectations. Scalability in other directions, such as a growing number of moments p or parameter dimension d, remains an important topic for future work. A complementary question is how to choose or construct informative moment functions to improve statistical 28 efficiency. Developing principled strategies for moment selection is a promising direction for future work.

Notations: We use the notations defined in the main text, and introduce the following additional notations used in our proofs. We use B(x, r ) to denote the closed ball centered at x with radius r. For a function f : Rd → R, we use ∇f (x) to denote the d-dimensional gradient vector of f at x and Hess( f (x)) to denote the Hessian matrix of f at x. We write N (F, d n, ε ) for the ε-covering number of a function class F under the pseudo-metric 

dn. For notational simplicity, we suppress the conditioning on the observed data X(n)

and write πα(θ), eπL ,α (θ), and eπL †,α (θ) in place of πα(θ|Xn), eπL ,α (θ | Xn) and eπL †,α (θ | Xn).Throughout the proofs, E denote the expectation taken with respect to the data-generating distribution P∗. When ambiguity is possible, we write EX∼P ∗ explicitly.

This construction leads to the following theoretical guarantee, which formally demonstrates the first-order control variate can enlarges the permissible region for the reference point θ†.

Theorem 5. (Intractable moment – First-order control variate) Assume Assumptions 1 and 4 hold and suppose (m ∧ k) ≥ nγ for some γ > 0. Then there exists a set B ⊂ X ⊗n with

Note that the tractable-moment setting is recovered by taking h(X, Z, θ ) = g(X, θ ) and letting k → ∞ . Because h is then independent of Z, the subsampled pseudo-posterior is independent of k, so k can be taken arbitrarily large. In this case, if ∥θ†(X(n)) − R θ ·

All computational runtimes were measured on an Intel Xeon Gold 6348 CPU. 

B.1 Bayesian Huber Regression 

We consider Bayesian Huber regression with parameter θ ∈ Rd, the associated moment function is: g(X = ( W, Y ), θ ) = ( −2Trun( Y − W T θ) W T , −(Y − W T θ)) T , where Trun( e) = max( −2, min( e, 2)) . We include the additional moment (Y − W T θ) to make the model over-identified, so that the number of moments is p = d + 1 > d , and to test the robustness of our method in the over-identified setting. The covariates X are generated from a standard multivariate Gaussian distribution with zero mean and an identity covariance matrix. The response variable Y is generated via the linear model Y = X⊤1d + ε. To test robustness, the noise ε is heavily contaminated: we concatenate ⌊4n/ 5⌋ standard normal samples N (0 , 1) with n − ⌊ 4n/ 5⌋ heavy-tailed samples from a Student’s t-distribution with  

> 4

degrees of freedom t(4) , ensuring the two noise components are mutually independent. We impose a uniform prior over the hypercube [−10 , 10] d ⊂ Rd. We compare the SMH algorithm with the full-data MH algorithm. We set the sample size to n = 20 , 000 and vary the batch size m ∈ { 200 , 500 , 800 , 1000 , 1500 , 2000 }.

B.1.1 Bayesian ETEL Huber Regression 

For the Bayesian ETEL Huber regression example, we set the parameter dimension to  

> d= 2

. We compare three algorithms: naive SMH with α = m, zero-order control-variate SMH (ZCV) with α = n, and full-data MH. All methods are initialized at the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimate. For the two SMH algorithms, we use the random-walk proposal q(· | θ′) = N  θ′, σ2  

> αId
> ,

where σ = 1 .64 /d 1/6. The full-data MH sampler uses the same proposal form, with α replaced by n. For the SMH algorithm, we fix the laziness parameter at ζ = 0 .5 and take the OLS estimate as the reference point. Table 1 in the main text reports empirical 95% credible interval coverage and interval length for the first coordinate of θ, as well as the effective sample size (ESS) averaged across all coordinates. All numerical results are based on 1, 000 independent replications, each with 10 , 000 MCMC iterations.

(FCV), and the full-data MH algorithm (denoted as full MH). We use the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimate both to initialize the Markov chain and as the reference 36 point for control variates. independent experimental replications, with each replication running 10 , 000 iterations of the respective MH algorithm. To study the convergence of the subsampling algorithms relative to the exact full-data approach, we track trajectories of the sliced Wasserstein-2 distance ( SW 2). Across 

30 independent replications, we first generate and fix a dataset, then run the full-data MH algorithm for 100 ,000 iterations in d = 5 and treat the resulting empirical measure as a benchmark posterior. On the same dataset, we independently run full-data MH, ZCV-SMH, and FCV-SMH from the same initial state (the OLS estimate). As the chains evolve, we compute the SW 2 distance between each running empirical distribution and the benchmark, and report distances averaged over the 30 replications. The results are shown in Figure 3a of the main text. To evaluate sensitivity to perturbations of the reference point, we use a similar setup on a fixed dataset in d = 5 . We first obtain the benchmark posterior by running full-data MH for 100 ,000 iterations, and compute the OLS estimate bθ.We then set the reference point and initialization to bθ + η 1d/√n. From this initialization, we run ZCV-SMH and FCV-SMH for 10 ,000 iterations, discarding the first 2,000 as burn-in. We then examine how the perturbation scale η affects the SW 2 distance to the benchmark, averaging results over 100 independent replications. To construct a first-order control variate, we must bypass the non-differentiability of the indicator function. We achieve this by introducing a smoothing trick exclusively for estimating the Jacobian matrix at the reference point θ†. Specifically, we replace the discontinuous sign function with the bipolar sigmoid function 

Sτ (x) = 21 + exp( −x/τ ) − 1.

This yields the smoothed moment function 

gτ (X, X ′, θ ) = ( W − W ′)Sτ

values, compared with Full-MH in a single replication. variate term; our target remains the Bayesian JEL posterior defined with the original (discontinuous) g. Consequently, the method is typically insensitive to the choice of the smoothed function, since the ZCV term provides a baseline correction as long as the Jacobian approximation is not too poor. Figure 8 illustrates this in a single replication by comparing the sampled density of the first coordinate of θ from FCV-SMH at several τ

values with the Full-MH sampled density. We can see even for a large τ (e.g., τ = 5 ), the FCV-SMH sampled density remains close to Full MH. We then fix τ = 0 .1. B.3 Bayesian ETEL for g-and-k Inference on Real Data 

We evaluate the proposed subsampling MH algorithm in a Bayesian ETEL analysis of the 

g-and-k distribution. The g-and-k family is a flexible class of distributions defined via a transformation of a standard normal random variable Z ∼ μz = N (0 , 1) :

G(Z, θ ) = θ1 + θ2



1 + 0 .8 1 − exp( −θ3Z)1 + exp( −θ3Z)

where θ = ( θ1, θ 2, θ 3, θ 4)⊤ denotes the vector of parameters: θ1 (location), θ2 (scale), θ3

(skewness), and θ4 (kurtosis). In particular, the τ -quantile of the induced distribution 

G(·, θ )#μz is given by G(qτ , θ ), where qτ is the τ -quantile of N (0 , 1) . We use p = 6 moments based on moment matching and quantile matching. Specifically, for i = 1 , 2, we set 

gi(x, θ ) = xi − EZ∼μz [G(Z, θ )i], and for i = 3 , . . . , 6, we use quantile-level moments gi(x, θ ) = 

1  x ≤ G(qτi−2 , θ ) − τi−2, where qτ denotes the τ -quantile of N (0 , 1) and (τ1, τ 2, τ 3, τ 4) = (0 .3, 0.4, 0.6, 0.7) and μz = N (0 , 1) . We use real heavy-tailed financial data given by the 43 log-differences of the CAD/USD exchange rate from the Garch dataset, denoted by {Xi}ni=1 

with n = 1866 . The parameter dimension is d = 4 . We impose a uniform prior over the hyper-rectangle defined by θ1 ∈ (−1, 1) , θ2 ∈ (0 , 3) , θ3 ∈ (−1, 1) , and θ4 ∈ (0 , 2) .For full-batch MH, we approximate the base measure μz by the empirical distribution of N = 5 × 10 6 draws from N (0 , 1) . For SMH, we vary the data and latent mini-batch sizes m and k. For the full-data MH, we begin with a 10,000-iteration pilot run using a random walk (RW) proposal N  θ′, Vn

> 

, where V = diag (4 × 10 −6, 8 × 10 −6, 4, 4) , and the chain is initialized at w1 = ( ¯X, S X , 0, 0.05) . Here, ¯X and SX denote the sample mean and sample standard deviation of the dataset {Xi}ni=1 , respectively. Discarding the first 2,000 samples as burn-in, we estimate the posterior mean bθ and covariance matrix bΣ with 10,000 iterations. These estimates are then used to construct a Gaussian independence proposal    

> N bθ, bΣ

> N θ′,Vα

To assess performance, we consider multiple configurations of the data mini-batch size m and the auxiliary normal sample size k. The results are reported in Table 4 in the main text. To evaluate the posterior inference accuracy and reliability of the proposed algorithm, we conduct a simulation study based on the empirical coverage of 95% credible intervals. We set the ground-truth parameter θ∗ to the Bayesian ETEL posterior mean, estimated from the main MCMC chain of the full-data MH algorithm in the preceding real-data experiment. In each replication, we generate a synthetic dataset {Yi}ni=1 with n = 1866 by applying the g-and-k transform Yi = G(Zi, θ ⋆) to i.i.d. auxiliary variables Zi ∼ N (0 , 1) , for  

> i= 1 , . . . , n

independent replications are summarized in Table 4 in the main text. 44 B.4 MMD-Bayes Inference

The term ∆4 does not depend on θ (nor on the mini-batches), so it can be dropped. The term ∆1 is computable in O(k2) time. The remaining terms ∆2 and ∆3 are the computational bottlenecks: ∆2 involves an expectation under μz , and ∆3 involves a full-data sum over n.A naive approximation of ∆2 would draw a large pool {Zs}Ns=1 

> i.i.d

∼ μz and replace the expectation by a Monte Carlo average, but this leads to an O(N k ) cost per evaluation. Instead, we apply an additional control-variate step to estimate ∆2 and ∆3 using mini-batches eXm and eZk. Moreover, this control-variate construction only requires the differentiability of the kernel κ(·, ·), but it does not require the map θ 7 → G(Z, θ ) to be continuous (or differentiable) for each fixed Z. Intuitively, even if θ 7 → G( eZl, θ ) exhibits discontinuities, the surrogate aggregates the corresponding contributions over l = 1 , . . . , k . This averaging across independent latent draws reduces the variance of the remainder term and makes the overall objective empirically more stable as a function of θ, provided k is not too small. For each choice of (m, k ),we proceed in two stages. First, we run the SMH algorithm using the naive surrogate in (9) (with α = m ∧ k) for 3,000 iterations, discarding the first 1,000 iterations as burn-in. 48 We take the posterior sample mean from this pilot run as the reference point θ† used by the ZCV construction. > 500

iterations as burn-in, with N = 20 ,000 . We then plot the sampled marginal posterior densities for each component of θ under each (m, k ) configuration (see Figure 9). As a benchmark, we run SMH with the naive surrogate at a much larger batch size, taking    

> m=n= 2000

, k = 5000 , and α = n, for 10 ,000 iterations with a 3,000 -iteration burn-in. This large-batch naive run serves as a high-accuracy reference for the marginal density plots. Overall, we find that m = k = 300 already yields marginal density estimates that closely match the large-batch naive benchmark, while maintaining comparable effective sample sizes (ESS). At the same time, the substantially smaller mini-batches lead to a significant reduction in per-iteration computational cost.

# C Proof of the Main Result 

C.1 Auxiliary Lemmas: Uniform Concentration for the Full Sam-ple 

To ensure stability of the full-sample objective and to handle possible non-smoothness of the moment function, , we work on a high-probability event where the full-sample empirical moments are uniformly close to their population counterparts. These uniform 49 concentration bounds allow us to replace potentially irregular sample quantities by smooth population-level limits (up to a controlled error), which is essential for subsequent expansions and for establishing stable behavior of the ETEL. The required bounds are summarized below.

C.3 Proof of Corollary 1 

Consider the set B of X(n) defined in the proof of Theorem 1. Throughout the sequel, we fix an arbitrary realization X(n) ∈ B , and (conditional on this X(n)) we work with the corresponding set A of eX(m) introduced in the proof of Theorem 1. We treat separately the exactly identified case d = p and the over-identified case d < p .
